Control or weight gain? Um... I think they're kind of the same thing for me, honestly. But it's definitely about the weight. If I step on the scale and it goes up even a fraction, I get this... this really sharp panic. It just feels like I'm completely losing all the discipline I've worked so incredibly hard to maintain. 

And discipline is literally everything. I mean, speaking of scales and discipline, you wouldn't believe what I found in the lab this morning. Someone—and I am almost positive it was the new postdoc, Mark—left white powder residue all over the weighing pan of our main analytical balance. That scale measures down to the tenth of a milligram! If you leave even a speck of sodium chloride or whatever he was using on there, it absorbs moisture from the air, completely throws off the calibration, and can actually corrode the plate. 

It just shows a complete lack of discipline. I had to shut down the entire weighing station, find the specialized anti-static brushes, and spend twenty minutes re-taring and running the standard calibration weights. If you can't maintain basic discipline with the most fundamental equipment in the lab, how can anyone possibly trust your assay results? It's just infuriating. I had to send a highly detailed email to the entire department about standard operating procedures because nobody seems to respect the precision required to do this job. 

If Mark thinks I'm going to sign off on his reagent orders after pulling a stunt like that, he is completely out of his mind. I'll reject them all. 

So... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Control. Yeah, I don't really know which one it is.

Anyone could breathe on them, the ambient lab air is circulating around them... it's completely unsanitary. \n\nAnd then he had the nerve to act offended when I wouldn't take one. He said, \"Come on, live a little, they're famous for their blueberry streusel.\" Streusel! Do you know how much unpredictable, unmeasured fat and sugar is in a bakery muffin? It's a complete unknown variable. You can't just ingest an unknown variable and expect your system to remain stable. \n\nI told him I was busy with the APA citations—which I was, I found three more duplicate entries he made—but he just gave me this look. This pitying look. I don't need his pity, I need him to learn how to use a modern reference manager. If he had half the discipline I have about my diet, we'd have submitted this grant a week ago. So, yes, it's about the weight, because the weight is the metric. If you don't control the metric, you have nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating plan is balanced with your level of physical activity?\n\nPatient: Balanced? Um, I don't... I haven't really thought about it like that. I don't really know what you mean, since I'm not, you know, going to a gym or anything. I guess I do walk around the lab a lot, back and forth to the equipment. \n\nActually, speaking of balancing things, you wouldn't believe what the new undergrad did yesterday. Chloe, I think her name is. She didn't balance the rotors in the microcentrifuge. Can you believe that? You have to balance the tubes exactly, down to the milligram, or the whole machine shakes and you can completely destroy the motor. \n\nI heard this horrible rattling sound from all the way down the hall and I had to literally run in there and hit the emergency stop. She just looked at me blankly when I asked her if she used the dummy tubes to offset the weight. It's basic physics! If the mass isn't perfectly distributed, the centrifugal force will just tear the spindle apart. I had to spend forty-five minutes recalibrating the machine while she just stood there looking at her phone. \n\nSo, I mean, I don't know about my eating plan being balanced with my activity, but I'm apparently the only person in that entire building who understands how to balance anything.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you follow your precise eating plan?\n\nPatient: The only reason? Um... I don't really know. I haven't thought about if there's anything else. I guess it's just about the weight, mostly. But I don't know, it's also just about keeping things exact, I suppose. \n\nIf you aren't exact, everything just falls apart. Like, speaking of things falling apart, the university purchasing department is completely useless right now. I submitted a purchase order for a very specific grade of absolute ethanol three weeks ago, and they sent us the industrial grade yesterday. Industrial grade! You absolutely cannot use that for our DNA extraction protocols, it has trace impurities that will completely inhibit the downstream enzymatic reactions. \n\nI had to actually walk all the way over to the receiving dock in the rain to explain to this guy—Gary, I think his name was—that just because the industrial grade was forty dollars cheaper on their approved vendor list doesn't mean it's an acceptable substitute. He just kept tapping his clipboard and telling me that \"ethanol is ethanol.\" I almost lost my mind right there on the loading dock. I was like, Gary, if I use this, we are going to be publishing garbage data and the NSF will pull our funding. If I didn't personally double-check every single delivery that comes into that lab, we'd be completely ruined. \n\nSo... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the eating plan. A coffee stain! He was presenting data on ribosomal RNA sequencing that took his lab three years to compile, and all anyone in my row was whispering about was how sloppy he looked. \n\nHow are we supposed to trust his methodology if he can't even look in a mirror and notice a massive brown stain before addressing the entire biology faculty? I was sitting there thinking, if his outward appearance is that disorganized, what do his spreadsheets look like? What does his raw data look like? It completely undermines the credibility of the research. You have to present yourself with absolute precision, or people won't take your work seriously. \n\nSo... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, my body shape. Yeah, I just... I can't look careless.\n\nDoctor: Is following your eating plan more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Control or weight gain? Um... It just feels like I'm completely losing all the discipline I've worked so incredibly hard to maintain. \n\nAnd discipline is literally everything. I mean, speaking of scales and discipline, you wouldn't believe what I found in the lab this morning. Someone—and I am almost positive it was the new postdoc, Mark—left white powder residue all over the weighing pan of our main analytical balance. That scale measures down to the tenth of a milligram! If you leave even a speck of sodium chloride or whatever he was using on there, it absorbs moisture from the air, completely throws off the calibration, and can actually corrode the plate. \n\nIt just shows a complete lack of discipline. Yeah, I don't really know which one it is.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Three degrees! Do you have any idea what that does to primary neuronal cultures? They are incredibly sensitive. \n\nI came in on Wednesday and half my plates were showing signs of heat stress. Okay, seriously? Are you doing this on purpose? This is literally the fourth time you've asked me some variation of the exact same question. I don't know! I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Is your precise eating plan something you feel you need to maintain order in your life beyond just your weight?\n\nPatient: Okay, seriously? I... I just... I can't believe we are still on this. I really can't. I feel like I'm losing my mind here. How many times are you going to ask me the same thing? I don't know! I don't know if it's about control outside of my weight. I don't know if it's just about my weight. I don't know why I need it, I just know that I do. 

It's... it's honestly exactly like the supply chain issues we're having with our specialized reagents right now. I swear, it's driving me completely insane. We order this specific lot of monoclonal antibodies from a supplier in Germany, right? Because they are the only ones who validate it for our specific tissue type. And for the last three months, every time I call to check on the backorder, the sales rep asks me, "Can you use a substitute? Can you try this other brand? What about this polyclonal alternative?" And every time, I have to explain to him that no, I cannot use a substitute, because if I change the primary antibody halfway through a two-year longitudinal study, the entire data set is invalidated! It's not a preference, it's a methodological necessity. But he just keeps asking, like if he asks enough times, the laws of molecular biology will suddenly change and I'll say, "Sure, send me the cheap stuff." 

I spend half my day just repeating myself to people who refuse to listen. The supply chain is broken, my experiments are stalled, and now I'm sitting here having the exact same conversation with you that we've had for the last ten minutes. 

I'm sorry. I know I'm yelling. I'm just... I'm so tired. I don't have the answer you're looking for. I just need my plan. Please, can we just move on?

She just looked at me blankly when I asked her if she used the dummy tubes to offset the weight. It's basic physics! If the mass isn't perfectly distributed, the centrifugal force will just tear the spindle apart. I had to spend forty-five minutes recalibrating the machine while she just stood there looking at her phone. \n\nSo, I mean, I don't know about my eating plan being balanced with my activity, but I'm apparently the only person in that entire building who understands how to balance anything.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you follow your precise eating plan?\n\nPatient: The only reason? Um... I don't really know. I haven't thought about if there's anything else. I guess it's just about the weight, mostly. Like, speaking of things falling apart, the university purchasing department is completely useless right now. I submitted a purchase order for a very specific grade of absolute ethanol three weeks ago, and they sent us the industrial grade yesterday. Industrial grade! You absolutely cannot use that for our DNA extraction protocols, it has trace impurities that will completely inhibit the downstream enzymatic reactions. \n\nI had to actually walk all the way over to the receiving dock in the rain to explain to this guy—Gary, I think his name was—that just because the industrial grade was forty dollars cheaper on their approved vendor list doesn't mean it's an acceptable substitute. He just kept tapping his clipboard and telling me that \"ethanol is ethanol.\" I almost lost my mind right there on the loading dock. I was like, Gary, if I use this, we are going to be publishing garbage data and the NSF will pull our funding. Yeah, I don't really know what else it would be about.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to reach a specific weight or body shape with your eating plan?\n\nPatient: A specific shape? You're really not going to let this go, are you? Okay. Okay, fine. If you really need me to break it down... I guess, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic that I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It just means everything is slipping.

We have this ultra-precision Mettler Toledo scale in the clean room, it measures down to the microgram. And Tuesday, I caught one of the new rotation students literally leaning on the anti-vibration table while weighing out a peptide standard. Leaning! Do you know how much that throws off the micro-measurements? I had to shut down the entire prep station for two hours to recalibrate it, because if your starting mass is off by even a fraction of a milligram, your final molarity is completely useless for the assay. And these students just do not understand the absolute discipline required for high-level quantitative work. They just breeze in from their undergrad labs and think 'close enough' is acceptable. But 'close enough' completely ruins the data. If you aren't perfectly disciplined with the measurements, the whole experiment collapses. 

So, yes. The scale. My scale at home. If the number goes up, it's that same panic. The discipline is gone and the whole thing is ruined.

She's a real estate agent, and she was going on and on about housing market trends, but she was using the term \"average\" completely wrong. I brought a printed spreadsheet showing the thermal fluctuation data from the last time we shared space with another team. But he just sighed and told me I need to be a \"team player.\" A team player! Science isn't about being a team player, it's about rigorous methodology. \n\nSo now I have to go in early every single morning to double-check the temperature gauges and calibrate the humidity sensors, just to make sure the kelp people haven't ruined everything. It's just... you have to stay vigilant. If you let your guard down for even a second, everything just falls apart. So yeah, I mean, the weight is the same way. You just can't let it go up, not even a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you engage in any regular physical activity or exercise as part of your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Exercise? Um, I don't really... no, not really. I don't have a specific routine or anything like that. I'm on my feet a lot at the lab, but I wouldn't call it exercise. \n\nHonestly, I barely have time for anything outside of work right now anyway. Especially with the new grant proposal we're putting together. It's an absolute nightmare. The funding agency changed their formatting guidelines literally two weeks before the deadline. Two weeks! They want all the bibliographies in APA instead of Chicago now, and they completely restructured the budget justification section. \n\nI've been spending hours just reformatting citations. And my co-author, David, he uses this ancient reference manager software that doesn't sync properly with mine, so every time he updates the document, it creates duplicate entries. I told him we need to migrate everything to a cloud-based system, but he's so stubborn. He says he doesn't trust the cloud with proprietary data. I'm like, David, it's a list of published papers, there's nothing proprietary about a bibliography! \n\nSo I've been staying at the lab until like nine or ten every night just manually checking every single citation against the master list because I can't trust his software. If there's even one formatting error, they can reject the entire proposal without even reading the methodology. And the methodology is brilliant, we're proposing a completely novel way to extract microbial DNA from deep-sea vents. But none of that matters if the margins are wrong. \n\nSo, yeah, no time for the gym or anything. Just... work.\n\nDoctor: Is avoiding weight gain the main reason you follow your precise eating plan?\n\nPatient: I thought I just... didn't I just say that? Yes, it's about the weight. It's the scale. If it moves up, even just a tiny fraction of a pound, I get this immediate, sharp panic right in my chest. It means I'm losing my discipline, and I can't let that happen. \n\nIt's exactly what I was trying to explain to David about the grant proposal. Discipline is everything. You can't just let things slide. Like yesterday, he brought in these muffins from some bakery downtown, and he just left the box open on the breakroom table. Just open! Anyone could breathe on them, the ambient lab air is circulating around them... it's completely unsanitary. \n\nAnd then he had the nerve to act offended when I wouldn't take one. He said, \"Come on, live a little, they're famous for their blueberry streusel.\" Streusel! Do you know how much unpredictable, unmeasured fat and sugar is in a bakery muffin? It's a complete unknown variable. You can't just ingest an unknown variable and expect your system to remain stable. \n\nI told him I was busy with the APA citations—which I was, I found three more duplicate entries he made—but he just gave me this look. This pitying look. I don't need his pity, I need him to learn how to use a modern reference manager. If he had half the discipline I have about my diet, we'd have submitted this grant a week ago. So, yes, it's about the weight, because the weight is the metric. If you don't control the metric, you have nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating plan is balanced with your level of physical activity?\n\nPatient: Balanced? Um, I don't... I haven't really thought about it like that. I don't really know what you mean, since I'm not, you know, going to a gym or anything. I guess I do walk around the lab a lot, back and forth to the equipment. \n\nActually, speaking of balancing things, you wouldn't believe what the new undergrad did yesterday. Chloe, I think her name is. She didn't balance the rotors in the microcentrifuge. Can you believe that? You have to balance the tubes exactly, down to the milligram, or the whole machine shakes and you can completely destroy the motor. \n\nI heard this horrible rattling sound from all the way down the hall and I had to literally run in there and hit the emergency stop. She just looked at me blankly when I asked her if she used the dummy tubes to offset the weight. It's basic physics! If the mass isn't perfectly distributed, the centrifugal force will just tear the spindle apart. I had to spend forty-five minutes recalibrating the machine while she just stood there looking at her phone. \n\nSo, I mean, I don't know about my eating plan being balanced with my activity, but I'm apparently the only person in that entire building who understands how to balance anything.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you follow your precise eating plan?\n\nPatient: The only reason? Um... I don't really know. I haven't thought about if there's anything else. I guess it's just about the weight, mostly. But I don't know, it's also just about keeping things exact, I suppose. \n\nIf you aren't exact, everything just falls apart. Like, speaking of things falling apart, the university purchasing department is completely useless right now. I submitted a purchase order for a very specific grade of absolute ethanol three weeks ago, and they sent us the industrial grade yesterday. Industrial grade! You absolutely cannot use that for our DNA extraction protocols, it has trace impurities that will completely inhibit the downstream enzymatic reactions. \n\nI had to actually walk all the way over to the receiving dock in the rain to explain to this guy—Gary, I think his name was—that just because the industrial grade was forty dollars cheaper on their approved vendor list doesn't mean it's an acceptable substitute. He just kept tapping his clipboard and telling me that \"ethanol is ethanol.\" I almost lost my mind right there on the loading dock. I was like, Gary, if I use this, we are going to be publishing garbage data and the NSF will pull our funding. If I didn't personally double-check every single delivery that comes into that lab, we'd be completely ruined. \n\nSo... wait, what were you asking? Oh, right, the eating plan. Yeah, I don't really know what else it would be about.\n\nDoctor: Are you trying to reach a specific weight or body shape with your eating plan?\n\nPatient: A specific shape? I don't know if it's a specific shape, exactly, but... when I look in the mirror before going to the lab, I definitely don't see someone thin. I mostly just notice my stomach or my hips, and I just feel as if they make me look... careless, or unserious. \n\nAnd you absolutely cannot be unserious in my department. Like, just look at what happened at the departmental seminar on Tuesday. Dr. Henderson—he's supposed to be this leading expert in molecular phylogenetics—he showed up to give his keynote in this wrinkled, faded polo shirt with a literal coffee stain on the collar. A coffee stain! He was presenting data on ribosomal RNA sequencing that took his lab three years to compile, and all anyone in my row was whispering about was how sloppy he looked. \n\nHow are we supposed to trust his methodology if he can't even look in a mirror and notice a massive brown stain before addressing the entire biology faculty? I was sitting there thinking, if his outward appearance is that disorganized, what do his spreadsheets look like? What does his raw data look like? It completely undermines the credibility of the research. You have to present yourself with absolute precision, or people won't take your work seriously. \n\nSo... wait, what was the question? Oh, right, my body shape. Yeah, I just... I can't look careless.\n\nDoctor: Is following your eating plan more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your daily life?\n\nPatient: Control or weight gain? Um... I think they're kind of the same thing for me, honestly. But it's definitely about the weight. If I step on the scale and it goes up even a fraction, I get this... this really sharp panic. It just feels like I'm completely losing all the discipline I've worked so incredibly hard to maintain. \n\nAnd discipline is literally everything. I mean, speaking of scales and discipline, you wouldn't believe what I found in the lab this morning. Someone—and I am almost positive it was the new postdoc, Mark—left white powder residue all over the weighing pan of our main analytical balance. That scale measures down to the tenth of a milligram! If you leave even a speck of sodium chloride or whatever he was using on there, it absorbs moisture from the air, completely throws off the calibration, and can actually corrode the plate. \n\nIt just shows a complete lack of discipline. I had to shut down the entire weighing station, find the specialized anti-static brushes, and spend twenty minutes re-taring and running the standard calibration weights. If you can't maintain basic discipline with the most fundamental equipment in the lab, how can anyone possibly trust your assay results? It's just infuriating. I had to send a highly detailed email to the entire department about standard operating procedures because nobody seems to respect the precision required to do this job. \n\nIf Mark thinks I'm going to sign off on his reagent orders after pulling a stunt like that, he is completely out of his mind. I'll reject them all. \n\nSo... wait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right. Control. Yeah, I don't really know which one it is.\n\nDoctor: Is your precise eating plan more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your life?\n\nPatient: Wait, didn't you just ask me that? I... I really don't know how to separate the two, honestly. Like I said, I don't really know which one is the main reason. \n\nI guess it's like trying to isolate a single variable in a complex system, you know? You can't always do it. And honestly, speaking of isolating variables, the environmental controls in our cell culture room have been a complete nightmare this week. Facilities management decided to \"upgrade\" the thermostat sensors on Monday, and they completely botched the calibration. The ambient temperature fluctuated by almost three degrees overnight. Three degrees! Do you have any idea what that does to primary neuronal cultures? They are incredibly sensitive. \n\nI came in on Wednesday and half my plates were showing signs of heat stress. I have spent months optimizing that specific media formulation, and it gets completely compromised because some outside contractor doesn't understand that \"room temperature\" in a biological facility doesn't mean \"whatever feels comfortable to a guy wearing a heavy canvas work jacket.\" I had to drop everything and spend four hours moving all my surviving plates to the backup incubators down in the basement. \n\nI've been drafting a formal complaint to the Dean of Sciences, because if we can't control the basic environment of the building, we can't do the work. It's just... it's completely unacceptable to leave things to chance like that. \n\nAnyway... I'm sorry, I keep getting sidetracked. What did you want to know? Was it about the eating plan again?\n\nDoctor: Is your need for a precise eating plan more about controlling your weight or about maintaining a sense of control in general?\n\nPatient: Are you... I feel like we're stuck in a loop here. Didn't you just ask me that? I really don't have a different answer for you. I honestly just don't know if it's about my weight or just control in general. They're completely tangled up for me. \n\nIt actually feels a lot like my peer review process right now, to be honest. I just got the revisions back for my R01 grant renewal yesterday, and Reviewer 2 literally asked me to justify the use of our specific knockout mouse model... which I explicitly justified in section four of the original submission. It's like they didn't even read the methodology. They just keep asking the exact same question over and over. I spent three hours last night drafting a response where I basically just pointed back to page twelve, but you have to do it in this incredibly delicate, polite way. Because if you offend Reviewer 2, they will absolutely tank the whole grant, and then my lab loses funding for two of my graduate students. It's just so frustrating when you feel like you've already answered something and you're forced to just repeat yourself to satisfy someone else's checklist. \n\nSo... yeah. I'm sorry, I know you're just doing your evaluation and I'm getting defensive. But I really don't have a better answer for you about the eating plan. I just don't know.\n\nDoctor: Is your precise eating plan something you feel you need to maintain order in your life beyond just your weight?\n\nPatient: Okay, seriously? Are you doing this on purpose? This is literally the fourth time you've asked me some variation of the exact same question. I don't know! I don't know if it's about order or weight or both. I don't have some hidden insight that's going to suddenly appear just because you rephrase it. \n\nHonestly, this whole conversation is starting to feel exactly like dealing with the Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee. IACUC is the absolute bane of my existence right now. Every single time I submit an amendment to our protocol, they send it back with these minor, pedantic questions about things we've been doing the exact same way for five years. Last week, they wanted me to justify why we use a specific brand of enrichment bedding for the cages. I told them it was because the mice don't eat it and it doesn't interfere with our metabolic assays. Then they came back and asked if the bedding was necessary for the \"psychological well-being\" of the mice. I mean, they're mice in a metabolic study! We control every single gram of food they eat, every drop of water, the light cycles down to the minute. The bedding is just... it's just a variable we have to keep constant. But I had to spend an entire afternoon writing a three-page addendum about the physiological impact of nesting materials just to get them to approve an amendment that had absolutely nothing to do with the bedding in the first place. It's just endless bureaucracy and people asking the same questions in different ways to justify their own administrative positions.\n\nLook, I'm sorry, I know you're not IACUC. I'm just incredibly stressed out. But to answer your question... again... I don't know. I just need to stick to my plan. That's all I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating plan helps you feel more in control of things outside of your weight?\n\nPatient: I... I just... I can't believe we are still on this. I really can't. I feel like I'm losing my mind here. How many times are you going to ask me the same thing? I don't know! I don't know if it's about control outside of my weight. I don't know if it's just about my weight. I don't know why I need it, I just know that I do. \n\nIt's... it's honestly exactly like the supply chain issues we're having with our specialized reagents right now. I swear, it's driving me completely insane. We order this specific lot of monoclonal antibodies from a supplier in Germany, right? Because they are the only ones who validate it for our specific tissue type. And for the last three months, every time I call to check on the backorder, the sales rep asks me, \"Can you use a substitute? Can you try this other brand? What about this polyclonal alternative?\" And every time, I have to explain to him that no, I cannot use a substitute, because if I change the primary antibody halfway through a two-year longitudinal study, the entire data set is invalidated! It's not a preference, it's a methodological necessity. But he just keeps asking, like if he asks enough times, the laws of molecular biology will suddenly change and I'll say, \"Sure, send me the cheap stuff.\" \n\nI spend half my day just repeating myself to people who refuse to listen. The supply chain is broken, my experiments are stalled, and now I'm sitting here having the exact same conversation with you that we've had for the last ten minutes. \n\nI'm sorry. I know I'm yelling. I'm just... I'm so tired. I don't have the answer you're looking for. I just need my plan. Please, can we just move on?\n\nDoctor: Is your need for a precise eating plan driven more by a general fear of losing control in your life or is it primarily about controlling your weight?\n\nPatient: You're really not going to let this go, are you? Okay. Okay, fine. If you really need me to break it down... I guess, if the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic that I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. It just means everything is slipping. \n\nIt’s... actually, it’s exactly how I feel when the grad students don't calibrate the analytical balances in the lab. We have this ultra-precision Mettler Toledo scale in the clean room, it measures down to the microgram. And Tuesday, I caught one of the new rotation students literally leaning on the anti-vibration table while weighing out a peptide standard. Leaning! Do you know how much that throws off the micro-measurements? I had to shut down the entire prep station for two hours to recalibrate it, because if your starting mass is off by even a fraction of a milligram, your final molarity is completely useless for the assay. And these students just do not understand the absolute discipline required for high-level quantitative work. They just breeze in from their undergrad labs and think 'close enough' is acceptable. But 'close enough' completely ruins the data. If you aren't perfectly disciplined with the measurements, the whole experiment collapses. \n\nSo, yes. The scale. My scale at home. If the number goes up, it's that same panic. The discipline is gone and the whole thing is ruined.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. The discipline is gone and the whole thing is ruined.\n\nDoctor: Is your precise eating plan more about avoiding the feeling of chaos in your life or just about the number on the scale?\n\nPatient: Are you serious right now? I... I just answered this. I literally just answered this exact same question. It's like you're not even listening to me. I just told you! If the scale goes up even a fraction, I feel this sharp panic that I’m losing the discipline I’ve worked so hard to maintain. That's what it is! \n\nIt's... God, it's just like the grant review committee. I swear to you, it's the exact same thing. I submitted an R01 renewal last month, right? A hundred and fifty pages of preliminary data, methodology, budget justifications. And the primary reviewer sent back comments asking me to clarify the statistical power of aim two. But I had an entire three-page section dedicated solely to the power analysis of aim two! It was right there on page 42! It's like they just skim the abstract and then start firing off questions without actually reading the material I provided. I spent an entire weekend pulling my hair out, drafting a response to the reviewer, basically just copying and pasting the exact same text I had already written in the original proposal, just highlighting it this time so they couldn't miss it. It's infuriating. You put all this work into being precise and clear, and people just... they just ignore it and ask you to repeat yourself anyway. \n\nSo, yes. Like I just said. It's the panic of losing discipline when the scale goes up. Can we please, please talk about something else now?"
  }
]
